## Part 12 — The Passage No One Could Sell

<!-- BEGIN 2016_CAUSAL THE_PASSAGE_NO_ONE_COULD_SELL_CHAPTER_15_NONCANON.md | git_blob 5bac0a5028acf452790c61ec41391660e86abb9e -->

# The Passage No One Could Sell

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XV candidate. NONCANON. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve canonical league-owner characters. Historical human Jake-to-2016 scoring-slot binding remains unauthenticated. This is invented secondary-world fiction, not testimony about a real owner or the historical cause of a fantasy result. The proposed quay, orchard, ferry, households, trade arrangements, geography, dialogue, and events below are not established Character Canon or Atlas facts. Set after the fictional events of Chapter XIV and the retained Week 13 historical notice, before any 2016 postseason character foreknowledge. Independent Authors Council, Character Canon, Atlas, Librarian, Umpire, and Founder-only promotion remain HOLD.*

### I. Beyond the sheltered road

The road became a different thing when it ran out of houses.

In the settlement it had been a measure of everybody's intentions. One could walk from the shelter to the washing place and know who would be there, who had promised a bucket, who was still owed a length of timber. Beyond the last whitewashed wall, however, the road rose onto an open shelf of ground. The wheel ruts separated. A footpath went through fern and returned at an angle that suggested the walkers had never agreed with the men who drove carts.

Jake stopped where the two tracks parted. The day had brought low clouds, though no rain. He had walked farther than he had meant to.

He had not left because someone at the shelter had sent him. That was the explanation he had wanted to give if he was asked. It would have made his departure sound like obedience to a useful request. Mara had, in fact, asked him to carry water before he went. He had done so. Afterward he said he was going to see where the carts turned now that the lower crossing was unsafe, and Mara had answered that he could see whatever he wished so long as he did not tell the people at the far end what they were obliged to do.

Her words followed him in the same irritating way as gravel caught inside a shoe. He had nearly turned back to object. Instead he set out, because he wanted to learn the shape of a road without first being told that it was his business.

At the rise he found a rough board nailed to a stake. The board had once carried a name. Only its last two letters remained legible. Beneath it someone had tied three strips of cloth, each pointing toward a different track. One had been knotted twice; the others were plain. The strips might have directed travelers, or warned them, or marked a child's game. He did not choose among them on the strength of their colors.

A man came up from behind with a narrow pushcart loaded with willow hoops. The hoops were taller than the man and rattled when the wheels crossed stones. He looked at the board, looked at Jake, and took the left-hand track without slowing.

"Where does that lead?" Jake asked.

"The cut."

"Does the road come back to the river?"

"If the river has remained where we left it."

The man kept walking.

Jake followed at a distance, taking the answer for what it was: not a guarantee, but a direction offered by a stranger with his own work to do.

The cut opened abruptly below a long bank of dark soil. On one side stood a row of fruit trees behind a low stone wall. On the other, across a shallow reach of water, were roofs that flashed pale where their slates had been repaired. Boats with flat sides lay nose to nose against a landing. Some had masts no taller than orchard ladders. Others were little more than rafts carrying baskets beneath oilcloth.

Nothing was deserted. Yet much of it had stopped.

Three loaded carts waited on the near bank with their shafts lifted and their animals unyoked. People stood around stacks of goods that should not have been left in the weather: wrapped bales, earthen jars, narrow boxes bound with cord, bundles of roots still clotted with their growing soil. A boy was arranging stones around the wheels of the foremost cart, one at a time, as if he could make it less likely to roll by making the stones beautiful.

On the far bank a woman in a blue coat argued with a broad-shouldered carrier whose boots were caked in the riverbed's red mud. They spoke across the water with the strained politeness of people who had repeated their positions too many times.

"The landing is yours to use," the woman called. "The orchard is not."

"We have no need of the orchard if you will give us the bridge."

"There is no bridge."

"There was a bridge yesterday."

"There was a bridge when the bed held it."

The carrier spread his hands. "I did not move the bed."

"No," she answered. "But if you keep speaking as though I did, it will not make the timber grow back."

Jake went down to the waiting carts. The first was loaded with sacks that gave off the faint dusty smell of seed. He knew enough to keep his hands from testing one. The man with the willow hoops had already gone among the boats, where two apprentices were measuring his work against a damaged basket.

The boy at the cart wheel looked up.

"Are you taking a load across?"

"I don't know."

"Then you're fortunate."

"Why?"

"People with nothing to carry can always say a road is too difficult."

The boy placed another stone beside the wheel and rose. He had dark smears on his sleeves and a serious, tired face.

Jake opened his mouth to ask what the boy meant. The cart's owner called him away before he could.

The owner was not the sort of man who mistook a stranger for a savior. He glanced once at Jake's boots and the empty space behind him.

"If you're here to work," he said, "there may be carrying. If you're here to tell us when the bridge will be mended, go talk to the river."

"What happened to it?"

"Water got under one pier. We pulled the planks before someone went through. The bank people say our animals cannot use their orchard steps. The boat people say they'll carry parcels, but not carts. The parcels belong in the carts. We've reached the end of our considerable wisdom."

Jake looked at the cargo. "Is it urgent?"

"Some of it. None of it improves by standing here."

"Can I help?"

"You can ask what needs lifting. That would be a beginning."

It sounded so much like the injured worker's answer at the mill that Jake almost smiled. Then he noticed the carrier's face and did not. The man was not giving him a lesson. He was trying to move goods before the weather worsened.

### II. The ground beneath the trees

The woman from the far bank crossed in a shallow boat, standing near the stern and controlling it with a long pole. She brought no passengers. When the keel scraped the landing, she stepped out and made the painter fast around a stone post, inspecting the knot before she joined the cart owners.

The blue coat had been repeatedly let out at the seams. Beneath it she wore a belt hung with a small iron knife, a measuring cord, and a narrow wooden gauge dark from handling. She could have been a boatwright, a keeper of land, or simply a woman who had learned that people listened longer when they could see she had tools.

The broad-shouldered carrier introduced her as Sela, then began his argument again before she had finished greeting the others.

"My people will take the steps singly," he said. "No cart wheels. We put down boards so the roots aren't cut."

Sela looked at the sacks. "Where will you put the boards?"

"Along the wall."

"That is where the roots are."

"Then tell us where they aren't."

"That is not a question I can answer by shouting across the water."

She led them through the narrow gate in the orchard wall. Jake went behind the owner of the seed cart, not because he had been invited to the discussion but because he wanted to see the ground they were proposing to cross.

Inside the wall the earth fell in two uneven terraces. The first held the oldest trees, whose trunks split into great pale limbs above a weave of dark branches. The second descended toward the water by a flight of stones set into the soil. The stones were dry in the middle, but their edges had shifted. One lay at an angle that made the step above it look like a broken tooth.

Sela stopped beside that stone.

"Last spring I had three people bring barrels down here. They used the wall for support. The wall moved. You can see where we packed it. We did not fix the root beneath it."

The carrier crouched to inspect a pale twisting line exposed in the earth. He touched the soil beside it, not the root.

"Could we carry everything by hand?"

"How much does everything weigh?"

The carrier named a figure, hesitated, and amended it. Another man objected to the estimate. Jake could not tell which of them was right, but the woman's expression told him the difference mattered.

"You are asking to carry a season's traffic through a garden," she said. "The garden is how my household lives. If the steps give way, your loads will be gone before we have another path to the water. My trees will remain here for me to explain to the people who depend on them."

"We aren't asking for a season."

"How many days, then?"

The men exchanged a glance. One said two. Another said until the bridge was restored. They did not know when that would be.

Jake felt the attraction of their argument. The goods had to move. The river was in front of them. The orchard steps were the only visible line joining the higher ground to the landing. If a path existed, why should it not be used?

He had begun composing a speech when he noticed a smaller woman seated on the lower wall, mending a fish net with an ivory-colored needle. She watched the men without entering their circle. At her feet was a wicker basket holding four loaves under cloth. She was neither a seller nor a petitioner; she was waiting for someone.

Sela turned to her.

"Your lower trees will take the weight first," she said. "Do you consent to testing the steps?"

The woman set down the needle.

"Not with those sacks," she answered.

"What would you permit?"

"One empty person at a time. Let them see whether the stones move."

The carrier gave a short laugh. "We already know people can walk down steps."

"You know they could yesterday," she said. "I would like to know whether they can today, before you ask us to stand underneath them."

Sela nodded as though the distinction were decisive. She asked a boat apprentice to bring a measuring pole. The men waited, impatient but silent.

Jake approached the loose stone. He could see a space beneath its outer edge and thought that if he pushed a wedge under it, the step might hold until the loads were down. He had handled enough timber lately to know where a wedge could go.

He bent, found a flat chip of rock, and slipped his fingers beneath the stone.

"Don't."

The word came from Sela, sharper than any command she had yet given. Jake drew back so quickly that the chip scraped his knuckle.

"What did you intend?" she asked.

"To settle the step."

"With a stone you found?"

"I thought it might keep it from moving."

"And if it carried the weight long enough for everyone to stand on it, then failed?"

Jake looked at the gap. The earth below was wet. He could not know what would happen.

"I didn't think that far."

"Neither did the people who built that little wall," said the net-mender. "It's still our wall."

He stood away from it. There was no answer he could offer that would restore the moment before his hand went under the stone. The old certainty that action was better than hesitation had become almost painful to carry. Yet he did not wish to replace it with the safer vanity of doing nothing at all.

The boat apprentice returned with the pole. Sela let the men examine the stone in turn, each from the edge of the upper terrace. Then she asked them to leave the garden while the household decided what could be carried, if anything.

At the gate the broad-shouldered carrier said to Jake, "You didn't make our case easier."

"No."

"Why did you touch it?"

"I thought I could mend it."

"Could you?"

"I don't know."

The man sighed. "There. Now we have two difficult things."

He did not turn the words into a judgment of Jake's whole life. He went back to the carts and began weighing the sacks with a simple hanging balance. Jake remained on the roadside, looking at the fruit trees whose trunks he had nearly made part of a bargain that was not his.

### III. A vessel in the current

Near noon a low boat came upriver with a broad stern and a patchwork awning. Its pilot stood barefoot in a shallow well near the back, moving her weight as though the planks responded to it. Two boys held the bow rope. On the boat's deck lay a broken wooden yoke and several lengths of line.

The pilot took one look at the waiting carts and called, "If these are all your passengers, the water will be here when they're finished arguing."

Sela waved her toward the stone post. The pilot brought the boat alongside with two strokes of her long sweep, then rested the sweep across the gunwale.

"You were told the bridge is out?" Sela asked.

"I saw it from upstream. The river has taken the footings and left you the appearance of a crossing. You ought to take down the sign before someone trusts it."

"The sign came down with the planks."

"Good. What is waiting?"

Sela gestured to the carts.

"Cargo," the pilot said, as though tasting a difficult word. "Not all of it will fit."

She stepped ashore and inspected the first stack. Her name, Jake learned from the apprentice who had come with her, was Ren. She did not belong to either side of the argument, though she had relatives who used the orchard landing. That alone would have made some people distrust her. She appeared to have no interest in improving their opinion.

Ren lifted the edge of an oilcloth. Beneath it lay boxes of simple clay lamps packed in straw, together with two smaller cases whose seals were broken from previous handling. She examined the thickness of the wood and tapped one case with a knuckle.

"Not this one," she said. "The bottom is loose."

The cart owner said he could bind it.

"Then bind it and let me see. Don't show me how confidently you can lift it."

She moved to the seed sacks.

"How dry?"

"Dry when they left the storehouse."

"When was that?"

The cart owner did not answer immediately.

Ren opened one mouth of cloth just enough to feel the grain beneath. She replaced the knot without taking anything out.

"I'll carry a sample over first. If someone is waiting to receive it, they can tell us whether they're taking the rest. My deck is not a warehouse."

"Can you take the whole lot before evening?" the carrier asked.

"Not by the measure of time you mean. My boat carries only what it can keep above water. Every extra sack asks something of the people standing beside it."

The men began calculating. Some cargo would have to remain. They named a fee, then another fee, then disputed whether there would be a charge for returning the empty boat. Jake listened until the figures became indistinct. Ren finally cut through them.

"I will take the lamps and the sound boxes on the first crossing, if you can show me people to carry them. The seed needs an agreement with the far bank. I will not put it aboard because you wish it gone."

"Who sets the order?" asked the owner.

"You do, among yourselves. I set what the vessel can bear."

It should have sounded like a refusal to help. To Jake it sounded instead like a place where help might begin.

He asked Ren whether she needed another pair of hands.

"Have you carried on water?"

"No."

"Have you carried breakables?"

"Yes."

"Do you know the difference?"

He considered. "On land, if I fall, I can sometimes catch what I'm holding."

"And on water?"

"I may be the thing that needs catching."

Ren gave a brief nod. "Stay off the boat until you know where your feet belong. Carry from the stack to my bow when I ask. If a case falls, let it fall. I don't hire hands to drown for clay."

"I haven't been hired."

"Then the instruction is even easier to obey."

The next hour involved more work than he had thought could occur in a place where almost nothing had moved that morning. Bundles were opened, weighed, counted and secured again. A man brought a length of tarred rope from a shed. Someone found two sound planks and laid them between the stack and a dry patch of landing. Ren rejected one plank because it twisted beneath a heel; she accepted the other only after both ends had been supported.

Jake and the boy who arranged stones worked beside the lamps. The boy's name was Bram. He knew the cargo by touch. He could tell a box of cups from a box of oil flasks by the way the packing whispered when the box was tilted.

"Did you make any of these?" Jake asked.

"My aunt did," Bram answered. "Some of the little ones. The rest came by road."

"Will she be angry if they're delayed?"

"She'll be angry if they're broken."

"Is she waiting across the water?"

"She's working. Somebody has to do it while we're waiting."

Bram hefted a shallow case and set it down again.

"You ask a lot of questions," he said. "Are you supposed to remember the answers for someone?"

"No."

"Then why?"

Jake looked across the river toward the roofs. He had no useful response. For weeks he had been asking people to explain themselves as if every reply could become a record he would be permitted to keep. Bram's aunt made lamps while he delayed in a quarrel beside the water. Her labor existed without his understanding of it.

"To learn what matters to the person carrying the box," Jake said at last.

"Then ask me where to put it."

Jake moved to the dry patch and waited for direction. Bram showed him which handles had been repaired and which seams could not take pressure. They made three trips. The boxes stood in two straight rows, but their heights differed and Ren altered the order before loading them.

The first crossing began with six cases and a bale of wrapped cloth. Jake stood at the landing watching Ren push away with her sweep. For a moment the boat drifted sideways, and the current took its stern faster than its bow. The boys caught the movement and shifted their weight. Ren said nothing. Her arms changed angle; the boat answered.

A patch of fast water showed itself midstream as a line of small standing ripples. Ren angled across it. One case tipped against another. The boy nearest it bent without being told.

Jake lurched toward the landing edge, half a pace into the water, as if he could reach the case by wanting to. Bram seized the back of his coat.

"She said keep clear."

Jake stopped. The water curled around his boots. In the boat, the boy caught the case by its rope and drew it back against the bale. Ren held the sweep steady. The boat went on.

Neither Ren nor the boy glanced toward Jake. Their attention had been on the thing they could affect. On shore Bram released his coat.

"She doesn't need you out there," he said.

"I know."

"You didn't a moment ago."

Jake stepped back from the edge, wet to his ankles. It was true. Knowing a rule in a quiet room did not make it his first movement when the world gave him a reason to forget.

The boat touched the far stones without further trouble. People there received the cases and moved them beneath a roof. Ren sent the empty vessel back across with a single passenger: a woman carrying a folded measuring chart.

The woman was not a herald. She was the person who would decide whether the seed could be kept dry when it arrived.

### IV. The cost of the shorter road

The measuring chart lay on an empty cart bed, held down at the corners by small rocks. It showed the water and the two landings in uneven charcoal, with numbers beside each stretch of bank. A line marked the broken bridge. Another marked the orchard steps. A third, newer line curved along the far bank toward a dry store with a sloping roof.

There was no line that solved the crossing.

The woman who had brought the chart was called Tavin. She worked at the far storehouse, which handled goods from many settlements and refused responsibility for anything not properly received. Her first question was not who had been delayed. It was whose sacks she was expected to protect.

The cart owner named his partner beyond the river. Tavin knew the name but said he had not promised space for the entire load. She would accept a sample and send word after testing it. He objected that the sample might not reflect the larger bags.

"Then we will have learned that from your bags," she said. "It is not knowledge I can obtain from your assurances."

He seemed ready to quarrel, but Ren asked him whether he wished his seed to spend another night in the weather while he argued about the limitations of a sample. The man looked at the clouds and yielded a small tied pouch for the trial.

Jake thought they had reached an agreement. Then the carrier produced a rolled strip of vellum, edged with green thread.

"If the orchard households permit a passage, here are the terms," he said. "The company will keep the way clear until the bridge is restored. All who carry goods may use the steps without fee."

Sela did not take it.

"Who wrote that?" she asked.

"Our keeper."

"Where?"

"At the high road."

"Has he stood in the orchard?"

"That isn't necessary to write a fair offer."

Sela turned the strip over without opening it. "It is necessary to know whether what you're offering belongs to you."

The carrier's face hardened. "We aren't claiming your trees."

"Then you do not need a document that says all people may use the steps."

He said the words meant only that no one would be refused on account of money. She said the words would still be there when the last loaded cart had passed, and her household would still be living where the step had broken. The disagreement had changed from a matter of freight to a matter of what people would believe they had acquired by moving it.

Jake looked at the two figures across the cart bed. At the shelter he might have tried to interpret one to the other. Here, neither required a translator. They understood one another well enough to disagree.

Tavin folded the chart.

"The dry store is not yours either," she told the carrier. "I can accept a limited delivery today if I find the space. I cannot grant every wagon on the high road the right to stop there afterward."

The boy Bram looked from one adult to another.

"If the words are what stop you," he said, "why don't you write fewer?"

Nobody answered. He flushed and bent over the box he had been tying.

But Sela had heard. She asked the carrier to read the proposed terms aloud without the flourish of his keeper's language. They began with one boatload. No route through the orchard. Only goods received by Tavin's store. No guarantee for future deliveries. Each owner remained responsible for his own goods until accepted. If the bridge was rebuilt, this arrangement did not become a right to use either household's land.

The carrier struggled with the last condition.

"How do we recover our expense?" he asked.

"By making deliveries that your customers are willing to pay for," Tavin said. "Not by selling them a path through another person's garden."

Jake watched the man consider this. His hand moved toward the rolled vellum, stopped, and settled on the cart's rim. At last he put the vellum away. He did not agree that he had been wrong. He agreed to send the sample and three more sound cases across the water under Ren's limit. The remainder would wait, covered, until morning.

It was not victory in the manner Jake had once imagined victory: a door flung open, a problem named and conquered, everyone free to move. It was the first movement permitted by the people who would have to live with its costs.

The carrying began again. Jake took a box of lamps from Bram, brought it to the bow, and set it where Ren pointed. They were lighter than the seed sacks, but the thin handles cut into his palms. He thought of the mill board, of how that smaller task had ended when the injured worker told him it was done. Here the task would end when Ren said the boat was loaded.

When the last case came down, a man from the carts ran toward them with another box balanced on his shoulder. He shouted that the space at the stern was enough for it.

Ren did not look at the space. She looked at the boat's waterline.

"Not this crossing."

"It's only one."

"Then it will be here when I return."

"You mean to charge for another trip."

"I mean to remain afloat on this one."

The man's eyes went to Jake, seeking an ally. Jake felt the old urge to support whoever sounded most aggrieved. The space at the stern did look large enough. Yet he had seen the boat shift in the current. He had no knowledge of its working load.

He moved aside so Ren could see the whole line of cases and said nothing.

The man cursed them both but carried his box back to the stack. Ren checked each fastening and boarded. This time Jake did not step toward the water when the vessel twisted in the shallows. Bram counted the cases aloud until they were safely across. They had carried less than the cart owners wanted. They had carried all that had been agreed.

When the boat returned, Tavin sent word that the sample had been placed aside for inspection. She would not accept the entire seed load without seeing its condition. She sent one canvas sheet to cover the waiting sacks, on loan and marked with her storehouse's red thread, and asked that it be returned whether or not the delivery went forward.

The carrier grumbled about the terms. Still, he helped stretch the canvas over the stack.

### V. The return not promised

By evening the river had taken the color of beaten metal. A wind from the open bank moved through the orchard leaves, showing their pale undersides. The cart animals had been watered and led to a patch of grass above the ruts. A few families from the near settlement came to collect smaller parcels, each making a separate arrangement with the people who had brought them. No one pronounced the road restored.

Jake had spent the afternoon carrying, and now his hands were sore. The owner of the seed cart counted out a small payment for his help. Jake said it was more than he had expected.

"It is what the work cost me," the owner answered. "Whether it was worth more, you'll have to decide by what you do next."

He did not offer Jake another day's employment. The weather might end the traffic before morning; the sample might fail inspection; the far store might have no space. Work that depended on those uncertainties could not honestly be promised yet.

Jake placed the coins in a different fold of his pouch from the money he had already set aside for the debt at the shelter. The new payment did not discharge that debt. It was merely the first money he had been given for work far from the people who knew the story of his mistakes.

He found Sela beside the orchard gate, straightening the temporary barrier the households had placed across their steps. The woman with the net was beside her, folding the last of the loaves into cloth.

"I thought you'd take down the barrier now," Jake said.

"Why?"

"Because the boat is carrying."

"The boat isn't the orchard."

He looked at the stones. The loose step had not moved. He could not know whether this meant it was safe; he only knew no one had loaded it.

"Will the path ever be open?" he asked.

"Perhaps to someone who has reason to use it and whom we invite. Perhaps we'll rebuild the stones. Perhaps the next rain will take the lower terrace. You ask as though a road is made once and then belongs to anybody who can remember it."

"What is it made of, then?"

Sela considered. "People returning. People repairing what their passage wears out. People agreeing where not to walk."

He almost repeated her answer to make it sound like something he had discovered. Instead he looked at the net-mender, who had stood at the wall all morning and whose household had the most to lose by the easy route. She was tightening a bundle with her needle thread.

"Was there anything else you wanted them to know?" Jake asked her.

She glanced toward the departing carts.

"Yes. My husband planted the lowest two trees when the ground was still wet from the last flood. I was the one who kept them alive through the dry months. The men who asked for a road did not ask which of us had done the work."

She said it without heat. Jake was struck by how little of her day he had understood even after watching her from noon onward. She had not been merely a witness to Sela's argument. She was one of the people whose labor made the place what it was.

Bram came running along the bank with a half-empty coil of rope.

"Ren says she can take one more passenger," he told Jake. "Across, not back. Do you need the far road?"

Jake had planned to return to the shelter before dark. It would be difficult now. He looked toward the left-hand track he had taken that morning; at this hour he could scarcely see where it separated from the grass. If he hurried, he might reach the high lane before night. If he stayed, he could help secure the remaining goods and wait for the next day's possible crossing.

Neither choice was heroic. That made it harder to choose.

"Tell Ren I don't need the far bank," he answered. "I'll help cover the loads, then find a place to sleep on this side."

Bram nodded and went back, apparently satisfied that a decision had been made.

The cart owner lent Jake a length of spare cloth and showed him a dry space beneath the outer roof of a store shed. He was to keep his boots away from the stacked willow hoops. The roof belonged to a household whose permission had been asked that afternoon, not to the carrier company. Jake agreed. The shed smelled of peeled bark and damp grain. He laid the cloth on a plank and sat where a strip of evening light came through between the boards.

From there he could hear the water, the occasional knock of a moored vessel and the distant voices of people who had not finished disputing the next day's work. A small place like the shelter might seem like an entire kingdom when one's days had never exceeded its boundary. Today he had seen trade brought to a standstill by the failure of one bridge and the refusal of a family to yield a garden. Neither was a small thing. Neither was a kingdom.

He opened his pouch and looked at the coins. He could carry them back tomorrow if the road allowed. He could not make the wet seed dry, the bridge sound, the daughter free of her father's shadow, or the injured worker certain of his hand. He had learned these limits often enough to be tired of learning them. Yet the day had given him something besides another account of failure.

Bram's aunt would go on making lamps. Ren would know by the tilt of her boat when the next case was too much. Sela and the net-mender would decide what their trees could bear. Tavin might refuse the seed, and the refusal might be the truest service she could perform for those who needed it. None of those people had waited to become part of his story.

The river made a hollow sound beneath the landing. Jake lay down without extinguishing anybody's light. Across the dark water, voices called the names of parcels as they were received, not the names of men who claimed to have opened the road.

In the morning he would have to choose again.

## Part 13 — The Work That Crossed Back — The Roof That Traveled

<!-- BEGIN 2016_CAUSAL THE_WORK_THAT_CROSSED_BACK_THE_ROOF_THAT_TRAVELED_CH16_V2_NONCANON.md | git_blob cb0e438cd6c38dff913023e90c8c18eb94833741 -->

# The Work That Crossed Back — The Roof That Traveled

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XVI developmental V2 candidate; original Chapter XVI preserved unchanged. NONCANON / independent editorial HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve authorized owner principals. The 2016 Flaim/ESPN archive does not authenticate the historical human Jake to scoring slot 1. This secondary-world river economy, its inhabitants, politics, choices, work and dialogue are invented; none is an approved Atlas or Character Canon fact. Story-time immediately follows Chapter XV and remains before any character knowledge of 2016 postseason outcomes.*

### I. What the river gave back

Jake woke to the sound of someone sweeping water out of a boat.

The strokes were short and impatient. Between them came the knock of the vessel against the landing, followed by a man's complaint that the rope ought to have been shortened yesterday. It was a peculiar way to discover morning: through other people's unfinished work, announced before the light had reached the crack in the shed wall.

He lay still and counted what he could hear. A cart animal stamping. The hollow scrape of a basket dragged over stone. The sweep of water again. No bell called anyone to breakfast here. The river had its own clock, and the people on the bank were already late by it.

The roof above him had not leaked. That was the first good fortune. The second was that nobody had taken the pouch beneath his head. He unfolded the scrap of cloth in which he had kept his earnings, touched the two separate folds and made himself close the pouch again. Money earned at the quay belonged to the same world as money owed at the shelter, but it did not feel the same in his hand. The difference displeased him. A coin was not absolution, whatever work had warmed it.

Outside, the three carts remained where they had stood when he arrived. One had shifted its wheels to firmer ground. Another had its shafts raised on a pair of stones. The canvas Tavin had lent, distinguished by a short line of red thread, lay over the waiting seed sacks. Nothing beneath it could be seen. By the nearest boat a boy was lifting a bucket too full for his arms.

Bram looked up at Jake and sloshed water onto his boots.

"You're awake," he said.

"That seems to be the case."

"I thought you had gone."

"Where would I have gone?"

"Home. You talked as though the road belonged to you yesterday."

Jake took the bucket's handle before it tipped again. Bram did not object; he was already reaching for another vessel. Together they poured the water into a channel cut into the mud beside the quay. It came out brown, though the river beyond the landing was grey.

"How much was in the boat?" Jake asked.

"Enough to make Ren angry. Not enough to sink it."

"Is she coming?"

"She says not to put a foot on her deck until she's seen the seams."

As though summoned by the warning, Ren came along the landing with a narrow lantern in one hand and a split wooden peg in the other. Her hair was bound behind her head with a piece of cord; she looked as if she had slept no longer than the boat. She gave the bucket an inspection, nodded once at the lowered waterline, and threw the peg onto a stack of damaged fittings.

"That thing was holding the after thwart," she said. "And it was the better of the two."

Bram stared at it. "It held yesterday."

"Yesterday it was younger." She put the lantern down. "I can take a passenger across once I've replaced it. Not cargo. If a carrier insists on making a grand procession, tell him the river has declined the honor."

Jake waited while Bram relayed the message to the nearest cart. He had begun to understand that Ren's jokes were seldom invitations to argue. The man returned in a moment carrying a small board and two pieces of scrap iron. Ren examined the board, shook her head, and took the shorter iron piece.

"That will serve as a measure," she said. "Not as the repair."

She went to the timber shed, where the boatwright's apprentice was working. Jake followed as far as the threshold. Inside were curved lengths of oak, lengths of line drying over a beam, and a smell of resin so sharp it seemed to clear the throat. A man with a scar across one knuckle stood beside a low workbench. He was shaping a peg slowly with a knife, letting each shaving fall where it would.

Ren showed him the split one.

"If you need it today," the man said, "I can make it. If you need it before breakfast, you should have brought it yesterday."

"I didn't know it would break yesterday."

"You never do."

The two spoke without quarrel and without ceremony. Jake saw the shape of their familiarity in the way Ren held out the scrap iron without explaining what she wanted it to measure. The apprentice took it and laid it beside a marked length of timber. He did not offer a completion time.

By the time Jake reached the carts again, a second boat had come up from the lower reach. It was larger than Ren's, with a wide deck and a high bow painted a faded yellow. Three men moved its ropes. The man at the stern wore boots with their tops turned down, the skin of his face grey with lack of sleep. He called across the water to the carrier company.

"Which of you is losing money?"

The owner of the seed cart lifted his arm. "All of us."

"Then one of you can afford to hire a pole."

A few people laughed. They laughed partly because the remark was good, partly because they wanted a boat that might carry more than Ren's.

The stranger stepped onto the quay and introduced himself as Deren. He ran cargo upriver, not across. His vessel had room for goods that could travel another day's distance, and it had work for hands who would load them before dawn. The bridge's failure had filled the banks with stranded labor. He wanted people who could be counted on to appear when the boat appeared, whether they felt inspired or not.

He looked at Jake's hands.

"You carried yesterday?"

"Yes."

"Paid?"

"Yes."

"Any trouble?"

Jake thought of the case shifting in Ren's first crossing and the instinct that had drawn him into the water. "Not of the sort that cost anyone a case."

Deren frowned. "You have a talent for making plain things difficult."

He named a wage for two days. It was more than Jake had received at the quay, and Deren named it in the same tone with which a man might state the depth of a ditch. No praise was offered with the work. No place beyond the second day was promised.

"Where do we go?" Jake asked.

"Up to the stone landing and back. If the channel stays open."

"What are we carrying?"

"Cloth and iron fittings on the outward run. Whatever is ready on the return. You don't need to admire the cargo."

Jake looked at the road that led back toward the shelter. He had meant to be home before this morning. Mara would have had his buckets counted by now. Someone else would be carrying them.

"When would I have to decide?"

"Before I find another man."

The answer stung him more than it should have. Deren was not trying to hurt him. A stranger with an open place on a working boat did not have time to learn the difference between Jake's old life and the life he might choose next.

### II. The lamps in the wrong room

Before Jake could answer, Ren's smaller boat returned from the far landing. Bram stood in the bow with a narrow note tucked inside his coat. Ren had repaired the thwart, carried Bram across to ask after the previous day's delivery, and brought him back to the near bank. The boy jumped ashore and looked around for the owner of the cart with the lamps.

"Three cases," he said, holding the note by its corner. "Tavin says three came into the dry store. The people waiting for the lamps say they sent four."

A quarrel began immediately, but it was unlike the dispute over the orchard. Everyone believed the lamps existed. Nobody knew where the fourth case had gone.

Jake remembered six cases and a bale aboard the first crossing, and the later load that had included lamps and sound boxes. He remembered the two straight rows beside the landing, the case Ren had rejected for its loose bottom, and the boy who had kept count as each load reached the far side. He did not remember four lamp cases being accepted by Tavin. He had not been across the river to watch them taken into the store.

"The fourth case is over there," one of the carriers declared. "They've put it aside and won't tell us."

"On what grounds would they do that?" asked a woman standing by the cart.

"Grounds enough to take our money."

Bram's face changed. He had been too young yesterday to command anyone, and now he was old enough to be blamed. The cart owner turned to him.

"You counted."

"I counted what went onto the boat."

"Then count what came off."

"I wasn't on the other bank."

"There are numbers on the note."

"Numbers aren't eyes."

The owner raised his voice. "My partner wants the lamps before the market closes. If we lose the day, we lose the buyer. I'll not be told it's a child's error."

Bram looked toward Jake. Perhaps he expected help. Perhaps he only wanted someone who had seen the loading to remember that the boy had done his work. Jake found that memory did not arrive whole simply because it was needed.

He went to the shed where the remaining cases had been stacked. The one Ren rejected was still there, its loose bottom bound with cord, the binding fresh and pale. Beside it sat a box with a shallow crescent cut near one end. Jake recognized the mark from yesterday. Bram had set that box down twice, once because its handle was weak and once when he showed Jake how to lift without leaning on the side.

"What's in that one?" Jake asked.

"Lamps," Bram said.

"Did it go across?"

"No. Ren wouldn't take it. I put it back."

"Then how many did the owner say he had?"

"Four when he left the workshop. I don't know whether he meant boxes or deliveries. He talks about both the same way."

The fact did not solve the missing case. It altered the question. The owner had believed four cases were meant to be delivered. Three had been accepted in the far store. At least one lamp case stood under their eyes on the near bank, prevented from crossing by its defective bottom. Whether it was the one the partner expected, and whether its contents were sound, remained to be established.

Jake asked Bram whether he could bring the owner to the shed. The boy took a long moment before nodding. He was frightened of carrying bad news even though he had not made the news bad.

The owner came with Deren behind him and Ren a few paces farther back. He saw the crescent-marked box and stopped.

"That was supposed to go yesterday."

"Ren refused the bottom," Jake said.

"Well, bind it and send it now."

"It has been bound," Bram answered. "But nobody's opened it."

The owner put a hand on the lid. Jake expected him to pull the rope apart without more discussion. Instead he knelt and examined the crescent mark. His shoulders seemed to settle beneath a weight they had carried longer than one night.

"My partner paid for the contents of that box," he said.

"Then he'll want the contents, not merely the box," said Ren.

Deren gave a small sound of impatience. "If a load is late, the load is late. Why must you make it a trial?"

"Because this one is made of clay," Ren answered, "and clay does not become sound by reaching the right bank."

The owner cut the cord. He lifted the lid and pushed the straw aside. Two small lamps lay broken along their spouts. A third had a hairline split from rim to base. The others appeared whole, but Jake could see the corner of a fourth buried under the packing.

The man shut his eyes briefly. When he opened them, he looked at Bram.

"Was it dropped?"

"I didn't drop it."

"That wasn't the question."

"I didn't see it dropped."

Jake remembered the box being moved from cart to stack and stack to dry patch, each time by hands that had already been carrying since morning. He remembered Bram stopping when the handle creaked. He did not remember any fall. His presence could not become a record of every motion.

"Neither did I," he said.

The man held the lid in one hand and the severed cord in the other. "Then we must go to the maker."

"She is here," Bram said, with sudden force.

Everyone looked at him.

"My aunt came down before light. She's at the wheel shop, borrowing a mold. I told her the lamps had crossed yesterday. She'll know which were hers."

The owner began to speak. Ren interrupted him for the first time that morning.

"Then go ask the woman who made them before anybody spends the rest of the day assigning blame to a boy."

Bram went without waiting to be sent. He ran through the waking market with his coat flapping at his sides.

Jake watched him go. He could have done the thing he had once been praised for doing: stand before the angry men, produce an account, and make their feelings part of his speech. He was beginning to suspect that the speech would only have delayed the arrival of someone with better knowledge.

### III. The maker's hand

Bram's aunt was called Iven. She came down from the wheel shop carrying a worn leather satchel and a length of cloth wrapped around a wooden mold. She was shorter than Bram and moved with the economy of someone who had spent a lifetime near breakable objects. A fine pale powder showed in the seams of her hands. She set the satchel on the cart and asked for the box to be opened again.

The owner told her about the lost buyer before she had examined a single lamp.

"I haven't lost a buyer yet," she said. "You've lost a morning."

"You haven't seen the note."

"Nor have I been invited to."

He gave it to her. She held it close to her eyes, turning slightly toward the light, and read it without moving her lips. When she finished, she placed the note on the lid.

"This says three cases received," she told Bram. "It doesn't say one stolen. It doesn't say one owed. It says three came in."

"I said that," Bram murmured.

"You said it loudly. I prefer to hear myself read it."

She removed the damaged lamps one by one. The first she could not save. The second had broken cleanly enough that the body might be ground down for another mixture. The third she turned between two fingers, inspecting the split.

"Do you see this line?" she asked the owner.

He bent forward.

"It's in the clay, not from the road. This one would have broken when it was lit. It shouldn't have left my table."

"Then that's yours," he said.

"Yes."

The word seemed to take the strength out of him. He had come prepared to argue with carriers, pilots, and storekeepers. He had not expected the maker to name a failure in her own work without a defense.

Iven laid the cracked lamp aside. "The two broken spouts might have met the edge of this box. The packing was poor. I made the third lamp too thin. We'll not pretend one mistake explains another."

"What am I to tell the buyer?"

"That three cases are across. That a fourth is here, opened for inspection, and some of it must be made again. Ask whether the buyer will take the sound pieces now or wait for a complete order."

"He won't like it."

"He may be right."

"What about the price?"

Iven looked at the sound lamps and then at the damaged ones. "You'll be paid for what you deliver. I'll replace the lamp that left my hand wrong. We can decide the two broken in transit when we've counted how they were packed. I won't guess before we count."

For the first time that morning Jake felt a strange impatience with the argument. It was not because he had found the truth. It was because the truth was emerging in so many small, ordinary pieces and no one was likely to praise the people who gathered them.

Bram stood close to his aunt, half proud and half angry. He had been blamed for a missing case and now seemed afraid of being excused as a child too young to be responsible for anything.

"I knew where the box was," he said.

Iven glanced at him. "Then why didn't you tell them?"

"I did. They asked me about the other bank."

"Did you answer that?"

"I said I wasn't there."

"Good."

He waited for the rest. None came.

Jake turned toward the river. Deren was preparing to leave. Men had begun piling iron fittings in the yellow-bowed boat, with two hands at each long crate. It was the sort of work Jake might do well if he paid attention: burdens with edges, quantities that could be counted, a destination and a return.

The owner followed his glance.

"Still want to hire with him?" he asked.

"I haven't answered."

"You're free to go."

The words were generously meant, or perhaps carelessly. They opened a hollow space in Jake. At the shelter people had asked him to help and then watched closely what he did with the request. Here an employer could offer two days and forget his name before supper if another man took the place.

He looked again at Iven and Bram. They were already repacking sound lamps for an honest partial delivery. Bram had set a piece of straw between two vessels so they would not touch. His aunt saw it and nodded. Their work had its own history; Jake had arrived only yesterday.

"How far is the stone landing?" Jake asked Deren.

"Far enough that you'll not walk home tonight."

"And tomorrow?"

"We'd return by the second evening if the water and the cargo agree."

Jake thought about Mara. He had not promised her he would return yesterday in so many words, yet he had allowed her to believe it. He thought of his unfinished pledge and the person who had lent him a dry blanket on another night. His absence did not extinguish those things. Nor did his return pay for them.

Deren looked at the sun behind the cloud and said, "You may decide while you stand. I'll decide while I work."

He went back to his boat.

### IV. A message no road could guarantee

Jake found a place beside the low wall where the wheelwright's apprentice had set out iron nails by length. He opened his pouch and spread a small piece of paper on his knee. He had not brought enough to write a letter properly. The paper was the back of an old tally slip; one corner retained a string mark from its former use. He borrowed a stump of charcoal from the apprentice, who gave it to him without asking a reason.

He wrote Mara's name first. Then he stopped.

An explanation long enough to sound respectable would require more paper than he had. He wanted to say he had found work, not abandoned the shelter, that the crossing was damaged, that the seed was uncertain, that he could earn twice yesterday's pay if he stayed. He wanted to say the wages would eventually bring him home a better man. The last part frightened him. It sounded like a bargain no one had offered.

He began again.

*Mara. I stayed at the river landing last night. I have been offered two days carrying cargo upriver. I mean to take the work. I have not paid what I owe. Do not count my hands for tomorrow's water. I will return if the road allows, and will not say the debt is settled until it is.*

The words were plain enough. He looked at them, wondering whether the most important sentence was the one about the debt or the one about not counting his hands.

A man who carried messages between the two settlements passed with a satchel across his chest. Jake approached him and asked whether he would take the note to the shelter. The man took it, read the address, and asked who would be there to receive it.

"Mara, if she's at the table."

"And if she isn't?"

"Leave it with the person counting provisions."

The man shook his head. "They've had words about letters left at that table. I can deliver it to the door. Somebody must agree to take it."

Jake felt a flare of unreasonable anger. The message carrier had no obligation to solve the problem of his uncertainty, but Jake wanted one thing that morning to cross a road without becoming another negotiation.

"Then ask for Mara. If no one accepts it, bring it back."

"I won't be returning to this quay until tomorrow."

"Then keep it until you do."

The messenger named a small fee, not a generous one. Jake counted it out from the new wage fold. He hated how quickly his money became smaller.

"Can you promise you'll reach the settlement today?" he asked.

"No. The high road is passable now. That can change."

"Will you tell me if it doesn't go?"

"If I come back and see you."

It was the most the man could offer. Jake let the paper go.

He watched the messenger put it into the satchel, where it disappeared among a dozen other messages whose senders were waiting on something they could not control. He had bought an attempt, not a delivery. Even if the note reached Mara, it might not make his absence easier.

Ren stood beside the boatwright's shed, testing the repaired thwart with the heel of her hand.

"How much did you pay?" she asked, having seen the exchange.

Jake told her.

"You could have spoken to a cart returning along the high road."

"Would that be certain?"

"No."

"Then I paid for someone whose work is carrying letters."

Ren looked at him as if measuring a knot. "Not the worst reason."

He waited for her to praise the decision. She did not.

"You're going upriver with Deren?" she asked.

"I think so."

"Thinking is poor cargo."

"I've been helping here. I didn't want to leave before—"

"Before the river was finished?"

Jake gave a rueful laugh. Ren did not join it. She leaned against the boat and rubbed a dark line of resin from her thumb.

"I need hands this afternoon," she said. "And I could use someone who remembers which boxes crossed without claiming to know what happened once they were received. I can't pay what Deren can pay. I don't have his distance to sell."

Jake felt the disappointment before he understood it. He had expected Ren to dismiss him for the stranger's better wage. Instead she was offering him a place in the small work he had begun yesterday.

"Would you hire me for two days?"

"No. I can tell you what I need today. Tomorrow I'll know when I see the water."

Deren's offer was longer and better paid. Ren's was nearer, with people he had begun to know. Neither choice would make him a better person by itself. The knowledge did not free him from choosing.

"What would I carry?"

"The sound lamp cases first. Then whatever Tavin accepts, if she accepts anything. I won't promise to move seed she hasn't tested."

Jake looked out at the larger yellow boat. Deren's men were already aboard. One of them called that a crate was ready for lifting and another answered that the rope was fouled. They were competent men, and the vessel needed hands who could learn their work quickly. There was no secret humiliation in becoming one of them.

He could see two possible versions of tomorrow. In one he would be upriver among men who knew nothing about the shelter, earning enough coin to quiet one small creditor. In the other he would stay at the cut, where his work would be measured each morning and might vanish at noon. The two versions could not be held together. The difference between them was not virtue but direction.

"I'll take Deren's two days," he said.

Ren's eyes moved toward the repaired peg.

"Then tell him now. A boat doesn't wait for a man to become certain of himself."

Jake reached the yellow bow before the next crate was hoisted. Deren looked at him as if he had been there all along.

"You'll carry that iron with the other fellow," he said.

"At the wage you named?"

"At the wage I named, if you work both days. If we come back early, you'll be paid for what we did. If the channel closes and we wait, there is food but no extra coin."

"Where do I sleep?"

"Under the awning if there's room. On shore if there isn't. Do you need a title as well?"

"No."

"Good. Lift the other end."

Jake put his shoulder beneath the iron crate. Its weight surprised him. Not because it was heavier than shelter timber, but because he had chosen to carry it and could no longer blame anyone for having been assigned the burden.

### V. The unfinished bank

The boat did not leave at once. A rope had twisted beneath a pile of fittings and had to be drawn out hand over hand. Another man discovered a seam in the awning through which rain had entered the cloth. The work was close and awkward. Jake learned to place his feet where the deck beam lay beneath the boards, to lift with the weight held near his body, to keep clear of the sweep when Deren turned the stern.

He made mistakes. Once he passed a line too quickly and it burned the skin at the base of his thumb. The man across from him swore, seized the slack and made him start again. Nobody explained the error twice.

On the bank, Iven and Bram were bringing the repacked lamps to Ren's smaller boat. Bram walked with his shoulders squared, carrying a case whose bottom had been braced with fresh wood. His aunt followed with the paper she had written for the buyer, not a claim that everything was sound, only an account of what had been inspected and what remained to be remade.

Jake called to the boy. Bram looked up.

"Are you going?" he asked.

"Upriver."

"When will you be back?"

"Second evening, if we can make it."

Bram glanced at the yellow bow, then at the box in his hands.

"That's a better boat," he said.

"Better at going upriver."

"You might see the old stone works."

"I might."

Bram shifted the box. "My aunt says there are kilns past the bend that keep heat for three days. She wants to know how they do it."

"Ask Deren when I return."

"Ask him yourself."

Jake smiled. It was the first exchange that morning in which he had not been asked to justify anything. The boy cared about a kiln because his aunt made lamps, and because a river that led somewhere might bring back a better method. Jake had no answer to give him yet.

Tavin appeared on the far bank while the lamps were being loaded. She raised one hand, not in greeting, but to signal that the seed sample had not passed its first inspection. The near cart owner shouted a question that did not carry across the water. A messenger repeated it from the bow of Ren's boat. Tavin answered that she needed another portion from a different sack before saying whether any of the cargo could be accepted.

There was groaning on the quay. A carrier stamped his foot and sent a pebble into the water. None of it changed the sample.

The man with the seed cart went back to the stack. He and another worker folded the canvas over the sacks more tightly and tied the corners to the cart bed. The red thread still marked the cloth as borrowed. The seed had not been moved, sold or eaten. Jake saw how much labor could surround a decision that had not yet been made.

Deren called for the last crate. Jake returned to his place.

The yellow boat pushed from shore under the effort of three poles. It was a slower departure than he had imagined. The bow kept finding the slack water behind stones and had to be eased away. One man cursed the current, another cursed the damaged bridge, and Deren told them both that cursing was a use of breath he would not pay for. They laughed, and for a short while the work was lighter.

The quay diminished by degrees rather than all at once. Ren's smaller boat remained in the cut, its new peg hidden beneath the thwart. Bram and Iven stood beside the sound lamps. Sela crossed the orchard steps with a shallow basket, turning toward the trees instead of toward the boats. Tavin's roof on the far side caught a narrow piece of sun. The high road rose behind it, leading toward a shelter whose door Jake had once believed he could keep open by standing in it.

He took the pouch from his belt and felt the difference between its folds again. His debts had not vanished because he was leaving. His unfinished promises had acquired a distance. The messenger might arrive before night or might not. Mara might be angry. Someone else might carry the water and discover that his absence made the work easier. He could not make any of those possibilities kind by naming them.

At the bend Deren asked him to help hold the forward line while the boat passed a line of submerged stones. Jake wrapped it around the cleat as he had been shown. The current struck the hull; the rope pulled hard enough to bring him forward on his heels. He set his weight back and heard Deren tell him to slacken one turn. He did, and the bow came free.

They were through before he understood how closely the boat had been held.

"That's the place," Deren said.

"What place?"

"Where a man who won't listen drags us sideways."

Jake looked at the water. It appeared no different from the reach they had just crossed. He would have missed the stones from shore. He had been useful because he had done what a more experienced man told him, and because his own arms had been there when the rope drew tight. Both things were true.

Ahead, beyond the bend, the river widened and a low line of buildings appeared beneath the hills. A plume of pale smoke rose over them. Jake could not tell whether it came from the kilns Bram had described or from an ordinary cook fire. He looked until the boat turned and the buildings disappeared behind a bank of alder.

He wanted to know. More than that, he wanted to be the kind of person who could go there, ask, and return with something worth carrying back. It was an ambition that had no relation to the shelter's praise and no defense against the shelter's anger.


### VI. The roof that traveled

Beyond the bend the air changed.

Jake noticed it first in the smell. Until then the river had carried wet earth, willow and the faint bitter smoke from distant hearths. Now there was a sharp smell like stones struck together. He looked up. The light had narrowed without his noticing, and the hills ahead had put on the color of old pewter. What little wind reached the boat came from the direction they were traveling.

"Rain," he said.

Pell, the older carrier handling the after line, glanced at the sky. "A weather report from a man who has been upriver half an hour."

Deren did not look up. He had been watching a narrowing between two shallow banks, his pole balanced against the push of the current.

"Make the cloth fast," he said.

The awning had been mended once already, but from beneath it Jake could see a seam that had opened again. A dull bead of moisture formed there and fell onto a board between the freight bundles. The first drop made a mark no bigger than a seed. The next found the same place. Beneath that board were tightly rolled lengths of woven cloth, tied in pairs. Iron fittings were stacked against the opposite rail and wedged apart by blocks of wood. Their own wetness would hurt no one. A wet bolt of finished fabric was a different matter.

Jake reached above the cloth to touch the seam. Pell caught his wrist.

"Don't climb over what you haven't helped secure."

"I can reach it."

"You can reach a great many things from a falling boat."

Jake stepped down. It was a small correction and a necessary one, though he disliked the sound of the men laughing at it. Deren called again to make the cloth fast. No one laughed a second time.

The rain arrived in a scatter of cold drops. Pell untied the edge of the awning from a post and tried to draw the slack across the split. The seam took the strain and opened another finger's width. Jake saw what Pell saw: drawing it tighter would put the whole length under tension.

"We need another piece," Pell said.

"There's a repair roll under the forward bench," Deren answered. "Short of a hand, long of a complaint, the both of you."

Jake found the roll. It contained a folded patch, two lengths of cord, a bent needle wrapped in felt and a little wooden wedge worn smooth from use. He expected Pell to take the needle. Instead the older man gave him the patch and pointed to a place on the deck where he could kneel without putting weight on the cloth bales.

"Hold it there."

"I could sew it."

"Could you?"

Jake looked at the needle. He had repaired a torn sleeve and once tied together a shelter canvas that had been pulled from its pegs in a storm. Neither attempt had required the cloth to resist a moving river.

"Not at the speed you'd want."

"Then hold it while I sew."

A gust turned the awning inside out at the broken seam. The corner struck Pell's face. He swore, hauled the cloth down and held it there with one elbow. Jake seized the other edge. The rain had grown thick enough to hide the nearest reeds. They could hear drops ticking against the iron pieces beneath the boards.

Deren swung the boat toward a low stretch of bank. The stern came close to the mud. A branch dragged across the yellow bow and scraped a streak through the paint.

"Can you work while we're held?" he called.

"If you can hold her," Pell answered.

Deren's reply was not kind, but he planted the pole and brought the vessel within the lee of a stand of willow. The current pressed one side of the boat outward. Another carrier stepped to the bow to keep the pole from slipping. Nobody asked Jake to explain the labor of those men. He could feel it in the movement of the deck beneath his knees.

Pell pushed the needle through the old cloth, then through the patch. The first stitch was ugly and strong. Jake held the two edges together while Pell made six more, each wide enough that he could feel the line tightening beneath his fingers. Rain ran from the back of Jake's collar into his shirt. The inside of his boots was beginning to grow cold.

"Another hand on the far corner," Pell said.

Jake shifted toward it. His knee pressed against the end of an iron case and the case moved. Not far, only the breadth of a thumb. But the wedge beside it tipped and fell with a hollow crack against the deck.

"Hold," Pell said, sharper than before.

Jake stopped. The word was simple. It did not require a bell, a proclamation or a judgment. He felt the instinct to put the wedge back before anyone could notice the mistake. He had nearly reached for it when Deren looked over.

"What moved?"

"I leaned against the iron," Jake said.

"Is it standing?"

"It shifted."

"Then leave the awning and make it stand. Pell can live with wet elbows."

Pell drew the needle through the next stitch. "Only just."

Jake worked the loose wedge beneath the case again. It would not seat because the floorboard had risen slightly under the load. He tried the wedge at another angle. It slipped. Behind him rainwater crept toward the bundles of cloth.

"Don't strike it," Pell said, seeing his hand close around a block of wood.

"I need to put it back."

"You need to know why it came out."

Jake bent to look along the board. The iron case had been set across a joint instead of between two fixed battens. Its weight had rocked against the moving planks. A wedge could keep it still in calm water, but the boat was not in calm water. Pell glanced at the case, said something brief to Deren and told Jake to bring the spare batten from the side rail.

Together they shifted the case while the third carrier braced its far end. It cost them time. A man on the shore, sheltering under the trees, called that they should have started earlier. Deren shouted back that the river could submit a complaint to him when it learned to write.

Jake placed the batten across the sound boards and watched Pell seat both wedges. The case stopped rocking. The awning still streamed above them, but no more iron moved when the hull took the next push of current.

"Now the patch," Pell said.

There was a dull scrape above their heads. A rope fastening the awning to the aft post had begun to pull through the damp wood. Pell's hand paused on the needle. Jake saw the narrow groove where the line had cut into the post, the pale inner timber showing through the dark wet face.

"That'll go," Jake said.

"It might."

"Can we use the iron tie?"

"It's holding a case."

Jake looked at the case he had just helped settle. If he borrowed its fastening, he would recreate the problem beneath another part of the load. He understood the choice a moment before Pell said anything.

"The spare cord," Jake said.

"Too short to lead to the post."

"What if we take the awning down from that ring and give the cord a shorter line?"

Pell looked from the ring to the nearest batten. The suggestion would lower the roof over the cloth but keep the pull off the split post. It would also leave the men working beneath it crouched like beggars under a table.

"Try," he said.

They loosened the awning's forward tie and lowered the edge by the breadth of a hand. The rain struck Jake's back while he pulled the cord through the ring. Pell held the patch close against the canvas. The new line reached. When they drew it tight, the post stopped creaking. The boat's temporary roof sagged, but the water began to run away from the finished cloth.

They remained like that for a long while, the boat held against the willow, all their clothes wet and most of the cargo dry. Jake thought of the covered seed left behind at the landing, the red-stitched borrowed canvas on a cart he could no longer see. He had wanted to believe that good work meant carrying something to its destination. Here good work consisted of keeping a thing from arriving ruined.

Deren stepped over the freight and inspected the case, the patch and the displaced fastening. He pressed a thumb against the repaired seam. Water ran past it and struck the outer rail.

"Will it hold?"

"It holds here," Pell said.

"Will it hold at the narrow reach?"

"I'll tell you there."

Deren regarded the altered awning. "We won't make the landing before the next meal."

Pell put the needle away. "We might make it with dry cloth."

"We might," Deren said, not pleased.

Jake watched the two men. Their argument was not about whether the repair was good. It was about the cost of making it. Deren had promised a rate for two days of work, not a share in what the cargo might earn. Jake had no authority to settle the freight owner's loss or the boatman's schedule.

"I moved the iron," he said.

Deren looked at him.

"I leaned against the case. Pell had to stop sewing and fix what shifted."

"Was it tied before you touched it?"

"Yes."

"Well tied?"

Jake hesitated. He could not know what the case had endured before he climbed aboard. He did know the wedge had been set against a joint that rocked under load. It was possible his knee had only exposed a fault already waiting for the river to find it.

"It was tied," he answered. "I can't say how well."

Deren waited, perhaps expecting an offer to pay for the delay. Jake could feel the few coins in his pouch, diminished already by the messenger's fee. He was tempted to make the offer because it would sound responsible and because Deren might accept it without ever establishing responsibility. His creditor at the shelter would have received another fine explanation for missing money.

Instead Jake said, "I will help secure every case again before we leave this bank. You can decide what the day's work is worth after we've done it."

Pell made a small approving noise that might also have been a cough.

Deren gave Jake a rope to hold. "Start with the case you moved."

They checked the iron, the cloth, the poles and the lower awning tie. None of the goods was declared delivered merely because it had survived the rain. They would have to reach the landing, where other people could examine what had been carried. One of the bales had a wet outer cord. Pell loosened it and separated the damp wrapping from the dry weave underneath. Deren marked the bale's binding with a scrap of charcoal so the receiver would know to look at it. He did not name the cloth spoiled. He did not name it sound.

When they pushed away from the willow, the river had darkened and the wind had eased. The roof over the freight was crooked, the yellow bow streaked, and three of the men were shivering through their shirts. Yet the cloth lay dry beneath its patched cover and the iron no longer knocked against the planks.

Pell took up the after line. He glanced at Jake's hands.

"You can finish a day's work without knowing whether it has helped," he said.

Jake would have preferred advice with a clearer ending. But the boat was already moving and there was a narrow passage ahead. His thumb burned where the rope had taken its skin. He wrapped his fingers around the line in the place Pell showed him, neither making a speech nor pretending not to be afraid.

The hills came nearer. Somewhere beyond the next turn, a place would be keeping its own count of the hours they had lost. Jake could not ask it to be grateful that the cargo remained aboard. He could only arrive with it, learn whose hands were needed next and take whatever account followed.

The boat went upriver. Behind it, the broken crossing, the orchard, the small rented shed and the waiting carts continued their own separate day. Not one of them was repaired by his departure. Yet for the first time Jake had made a choice whose cost he could not discharge by arriving home with an explanation.

He tightened the line when Deren called for it. The river carried them around the next bend.

---
